Despite the degradation of global growth expectations, global property prices are still high. The banks, whose balance sheets have already weakened due to the global crisis, are now facing significant losses due to the declines in the market value of the problem country's bonds they keep in their portfolios. Advanced countries' growth under the potential for post-crisis growth and lack adequate employment affect global growth prospects. It is also possible that this problem in developed countries, which make up more than half of the world's economy, will also have a negative impact on developing countries through trade and capital channel. In addition, the failure of developed countries to come up with a reliable mid-term financial plan has negative impact on financial markets and the expectations of investors and consumers. Mr. President, the esteemed MPs, as you know, the global crisis has shown its most destructive effect on employment. Since 2007, there have been more than 26 million unemployed in the world. The recovery of the global economy, the first half of 2010 and 2011, unfortunately did not compensate for the loss of employment. The unemployment rates are still above pre-crisis levels. As of October 2011, unemployment has increased to 180 in the United States and 140 in the developed and eurozone countries. In creating employment, Turkey is separated from the world in a positive direction. Thanks to the active labour force policies and strong growth we put into practice, Turkey has created record jobs. Mr. President, valued lawmakers today, developed countries face the issue of sustainability of debt. In the developed countries, the general state budget deficit, which averaged 1.1 per cent during the pre-crisis period, increased to eight to nine times in 2009 and to 8.7 per cent in 2010, up to 7.5 per cent. In 2011-2012, it is estimated to be 6.5 per cent and 5.2%. Similarly, in the Eurozone, the GDP deficit, which was only 7 per thousand in the pre-crisis period, increased to 6.3 per cent and 6 per cent in 2009 and 2010, respectively. It is estimated to fall to 4.1 per cent and 3.1 per cent, respectively, in 2011 and 2012, as a result of the measures taken. In particular, rising budget deficits in developed countries have also caused the public debt supply to increase. During 2007-2011, the GDP of public debt in developed countries increased by 30 points to 104 per cent. The average debt supply in the eurozone is expected to increase from 66.4% in 2007 to 88.6% in 2011 and 90 per cent in 2012. Academic studies have shown that a 90 per cent public debt stock has had a very serious negative impact on long-term growth rates. In developing countries, public financing balances have a healthier outlook. These countries have reduced budget deficits due to post-crisis financial constraints, even though they have given a budget deficit along with the crisis. In the pre-crisis period, the developing countries gave 1.2 per cent of the state's GDP surplus, with 4.1 per cent and 2.9 per cent deficit in 2009 and 2010 respectively. The general state deficit in the developed countries is expected to be slightly below the GDP rate this year and next year. The developing countries are much better off than those developed in debt stocks. The debt stock in these countries, which was around 35 per cent prior to the crisis, is expected to reduce to around 36 per cent and 35 per cent in 2009 and 2010, as well as 35 per cent in 2010, respectively, to around 35% and 39 per cent in 2011 and 2012. In Turkey, we have achieved pre-crisis levels in our financial balances thanks to the right policies we have implemented. Our country's general state budget deficit, which was 0.2% before the crisis, increased to 5.5 per cent of the GDP, with the impact of the global crisis first, but due to measures taken by our government, the budget deficit, reduced to 2.9 per cent in 2010, is estimated to drop to around 1% in 2011. And we predict that this is going to happen at 0.8% in 2012. Similarly, Turkey's gross public debt stock, which was about 40.9 per cent prior to the crisis, increased to 46.1 per cent in 2009, with its GDP in general with the crisis, but due to budget discipline, it declined to 42.2 per cent in 2010, which we estimate will drop to 39.8 per cent in 2011, to 37 per cent in 2012. In 2014, the end of the programme period, we aim to reduce Turkey's gross public debt supply rate to 32 per cent. Mr. President, valuable lawmakers have seen an increase in inflation in developing countries along with economic recovery and high commodity prices following the global crisis. Inflation in developing countries, which were 6.7 per cent in 2010, is estimated to rise to 7 per cent in 2011. In 2012, inflation was reduced to 5.5 per cent in developing countries, with the prospect of slowing down and softening proportions in global economic prices. The recent increase in inflation in Turkey is, of course, not unique to us. For example, inflation was 7.2% in Brazil, 7.2% in Russia; 9.4% in India; 9.7% in Argentina. In 2010, inflation at the end of the year, which is 6.4 per cent, is expected to rise to 8.3 per cent by the end of 2011, according to the National Bank estimates. In addition to the increase in the commodity prices mentioned earlier, strong domestic demand, the loss of value in Turkish lira and the increase in taxes in October have been effective. Given that the internal demand for deceleration and the effects of external factors pushing inflation upwards will be temporary, we estimate inflation will approach a five per cent target in 2012. The strong performance of the Turkish economy is not a coincidence. Despite the largest global crisis of the last 60 years in 2009, Turkey's economy grew 5.1 per cent during the EC PARTY administrations -- from 2003 to 2011. Mr. President, valued lawmakers, one of the most striking features of post-crisis economic recovery is a growth that creates high employment. The unemployment rate, which was 10.3 per cent in 2007, rose to 16.1 per cent in February 2009, but was reduced to 9.2 per cent of unemployment as of August 2011, due to the active labour force policies we implemented and the strong performance we achieved in growth. Thus, Turkey has been one of the few countries to reduce unemployment to pre-crisis. The seasonal-free unemployment rate has declined by 9.6 per cent as of August to the lowest since 2005, when this series began to be held. Mr. President, the Turkish banking sector is too healthy to compare with many countries around the world these days when the eurozone public debt crisis threatens the global banking system. First of all, the capital structure of our banking sector is very strong. During the period of our governments, the Turkish banking sector's resources have increased by about five times its capital from $26 billion to $142 billion as of September 2011. Our banking system has a capital efficiency rate of 16.4 per cent and more than 2 per cent of the minimum legal limit. Second, the active quality of our banking sector is very high. As of September 2011, the total rate of problem loans has been reduced to 2.7 per cent; that rate was around 17 per cent in 2002. Third, during the 2007-2010 period, when many banks sank in the world, our banking sector average annual self-source profit was 21.6 per cent. So banks have made almost $22 against their $100 capital at this time. During this period, we also limit the distribution of profits to banks' shareholders to protect the strong self-source structure of the banking sector. Prior to EC PARTY governments, the weak banking sector has not only prevented economic stability programmes from succeeding, but has been a key factor in triggering the 1994 and 2001 crises. Today, however, the banking sector has been the most important support for Turkey's strong output in the post-global crisis period. We are one of the few countries in a crisis where there are no bankrupt banking problems and in this sense no burden on their citizens, because the banking sector, which many developed and developing countries have only started in the post-crisis period, has started doing stress tests since 2004. Again, after the global crisis, we took steps in 2006 on the need for high capital against risks, and we launched a 12 per cent target capital efficiency rate. Mr. President, valued lawmakers, one of the key factors that separates Turkey from other countries at a time when global uncertainty is increasing is the balance sheet of powerful households. In our country, the debt level of the household has risen in recent years, but is still relatively low. More importantly, the ability of the household to return its debt has increased. Before the crisis, 12.3% of the household's obligations reached 18.8% of the GDP as of June 2011. This is an average of 60 per cent in the European Union-27, with many developing countries within it. On the other hand, the risk of interest for households is also low because almost all of their loans are fixed interest. Finally, the households have little to say that there is no risk of courting on their balance sheet, because our government does not allow the household to borrow foreign currency as a measure of macro-capitality. In 2002, the share of foreign currency and foreign currency-related loans in consumer loans was 15 per cent, while as of September 2011, the rate was down to 1 per cent. Mr. President, high-value lawmakers, despite a record rise in employment in recent years, unemployment is still not at the level we desire. This is due to some structural properties of our labour market. Now, let me share these features with you and explain what we do as governments to overcome these problems, structural problems: First, the population growth in our country is high for many countries, while the turnout in the labour force is low. For example, if you take into account the increase in working-age population over the last year, let's say 800,000 people -- because at a low level -- 1.1 million people have somehow entered the labour market. The young population and the rapidly increasing turnout of the labour force make it difficult to lower the unemployment rate, but, as you know, we succeeded. Second, compared to developed countries, our country's population of agricultural workers has a high share of employment. Despite the decline of recent years, one-quarter of the population in Turkey has been employed in the agricultural sector, or so it seems. The fact that such a large part of Turkey's population is employed in agriculture also points to secret unemployment in a sense. However, the total employment share of the agricultural population is 1.6% in the United States, 3.6 per cent in the eurozone, and the OECD, with 34 members on the average, is 5.1. So the transition from agriculture to other sectors is expected to continue in the coming years. This transformation is a factor that makes it difficult to lower unemployment rates in Turkey. On the other hand, I would like to share with you the issue of increased employment quality since 2002. The share of free family labor is actually reduced from 21 per cent in 2002 to 15 per cent in August 2011, so the number of undercover unemployed has been reduced. Third, our population over the age of twenty-five has a low average of education. By the way, I'll tell you, our population over the age of twenty-five is six and a half years in an average school, so that's middle abandonment. The intense transition from agriculture to non-advised sectors has led to the supply and demand discrepancies in the labour market. Nearly two thirds of the total labour force in Turkey -- as I have just expressed -- have six upper secondary educations. I would like to stress that these issues reveal the importance of education and vocational education in reducing unemployment. Finally, one of the most important factors limiting employment in our country is rigor in the labour market. Turkey is the last of 34 OECD members in terms of labour market flexibility indicators. Therefore, the average working period in Turkey is very high, forty-nine hours a week. In addition, only 11.5 per cent of the total employment is part-time, while the average rate in OECD is 16.6 per cent. To bring Turkey's economy to its job-oriented goal of sustainable growth, we will continue to focus on the workforce quality, the flexibility of the labour market and policies that will boost participation in the labour force. Our mid-term programme adopted a holistic approach to solving the unemployment problem and presented short, medium and long-term measures and policies aimed at reducing unemployment. One of the most effective ways to increase employment in the short term is to implement active labour market policies. That's what we've done. We've been working very hard in recent years to make the workforce qualified. For the past three years, we've been implementing active labor force programs nationwide. The number of users of these programmes increased in 2008 to 212,000 in 2010, while more than 200,000 of our citizens took advantage of the programme by the end of October 2011. This, of course, is an important step in the short term, an important step forward; we took it. In the middle term, we have implemented many measures in a similar way to increase employment. In the beginning, in 2009, we provided 9.616 incentives with a new investment incentive system. With these incentive documents, there will be an investment of about 133 billion dollars, and with these investments there will be 322,000 jobs, so there will be investments, there will be production, there will be employment. What did we do about it? We rationalized the incentive system. Also, with a medium-term perspective, our government has decided to allocate more resources to regional development projects, especially GAP, DAP, DAP, DOCAP, all of them, and in recent years, these resources have been significantly increased. Among these projects are the potential for employment, both for the region and for the country's economy. According to international studies, employment is created directly and indirectly for two people per hectare, along with the transition to watery agriculture. In our country, of course, water is intended to be delivered to 1,88,000 hectares in the GAP region in the coming period. tbmm-2011-12-08sit01spe0008par0203	Thank you, Mr. President. Dear MPs, budgets are important documents, not just for our country, but for all countries, whether their democracy has improved or not. On the budgets, the government sees criticism in the eyes of the opposition, and in that frame, it examines events, responds to criticism, reviews how the opposition sees things, listens to them personally. Of course, in this budget, we'd like our Prime Minister to be here and listen to our criticism. I ask God to cure him for his illness. I hope he gets well soon and resumes his duties as Prime Minister in Parliament. I called him on the phone, sent him my best wishes, my best wishes. On this occasion, I consider it a duty to transfer once again. My dear friends, we said the budgets were important. The importance of budgets comes from where taxes are collected and how they are spent as the will of Parliament. If every citizen in this country pays taxes from the moment they're born, he has to see where their taxes are being spent, he has to know, and he has to ask for his account. The reason for democracy, the reason for freedoms, the reason for its emergence, is because it is being asked about the accounts of taxes paid. And another important thing is that in each country's constitution, apart from the process of making normal laws, there are special provisions for the production of budgets. We have special provisions, we have special regulations, we have a special calendar, when the government will send the budget to Parliament, we have how to negotiate, and therefore the budget law is an important law. There's another thing that's important: it's the procedure of the budget to prepare. Unlike other laws, all ministries, independent organisations include all the institutions of the public in this process and all these processes are evaluated while the budget is being prepared, while the budget is being negotiated. I don't know about my friends coming to Parliament this semester, but I did say something to my friends from last semester. There is a law called the Law on Financial Management and Control, which is called the Constitutional Constitution. This Law came out in Parliament in the last legislature because the country needed a new fiscal structure, and according to this Law, the Central Time Program must be announced by the end of May, and the Central Financial Plan must be announced by June 15th at the latest so that budgets can be made healthy. Now think of a government that says you care about the budget; it's good. What does it mean to care about the budget? That means following the laws of Parliament. If a government doesn't obey the laws of parliament, how in God's name are we supposed to expect a citizen to obey the law? I'll give you an example: A hundred and thirty-five days after the time appointed by the Central Time Program Law, a full hundred and twenty days after the Central Timed Financial Plan, the Budget Call and Budget Prepping Guide and the Investment General and the Investment Programme were all merged on 13 October 2011 and published in the Official Gazette. As a person who's worked for the Treasury for many years, we know how budgets are prepared, there are articles coming in, there are preparations being made according to those articles. Imagine, you do not obey your own laws, you publish them all in one day, and you send them to Parliament as if the budget was ready on the 13th and 17th. How do we trust who, and how do we see the sincerity of this budget? How can we be sure of the sincerity of this budget if a government does not publish the Central Term Financial Plan on time? Therefore, the government has set a record of failing to abide by its own laws, no matter how proud it is. But it has to be written in a corner in Turkey as a disgrace to the history of law. There's chaos in economic management, friends, and there's a lot of faith. Look, I'll give you a lot of examples: there's the Ministry of Economy, there's the Ministry of Development, there's the Deputy Prime Minister in Charge of Treasury, there's the Ministry of Finance, there's the Ministry of Science, Industry and Technology, there's the Ministry of Customs and Trade. The plurality is not only limited to these things, but also reflected in the words. I'll give you an example of the economic crisis: Mr. Prime Minister gave a speech at the Turkish Exporters' Assembly, and there's a crisis in which to pass them on, friends. Turkey is now on solid ground, and we have nothing to do with the crisis. The Prime Minister says it's his opinion, June 26th. And then Mr. Vice President of the AKP goes on TV and explains: "The economic crisis is on its way, don't spend too much money. Who are we going to believe, the Prime Minister or the Vice President? Then another Minister comes out, Mr. Cavalan says, "We don't have a suggestion that we can't spend it on the town." Who do we believe? One says, "Don't waste it." The other says, "We don't want to waste it." And again, when the Prime Minister presented the government programme, he said that the economy would continue to cool down. So we take precautions, we keep cooling the economy. Well, that means we don't have anything to do with the crisis. It's not the right thing to say. And, Mr. Babacan, three months ago, we gave instructions to relevant institutions, asking them to be ready for all bad scenarios. And Mr. Prime Minister says the waved courtship regime will continue. One of the most important reasons why the deficit is a problem is that Turkish lira is extremely valuable, so it is against it. One of the important essential elements of our program is said to be a trick-or-treater. There's another minister who says we're going to solve the deficit by applying a strict monetary policy. Another minister is coming out, saying Cari is not a danger to Turkey. It prevents healthy decisions from being made. My dear friends, why should we raise the budget?
